It's actually a huge problem in city water mains. They have to send these special robots down into the pipes to scrape them clean. I saw a documentary about it on YouTube. The robots have these rotating wire brushes... but yeah, I don't think I'm depressed all the time. I mean, I get anxious if I have a big presentation at work or something, but not just out of nowhere. I had to do a presentation last month on the tensile strength of this new carbon fiber weave we were testing. Carbon fiber is so tricky to work with because if you don't cure the epoxy resin at the exact right temperature, it gets brittle. We have this massive industrial oven just for curing it. It looks like a giant pizza oven, honestly. It actually made me crave pizza the whole time I was running the tests. I ended up ordering from this local place down the street, but they use way too much cornmeal on the bottom of the crust. It gets all over your hands and the table. I prefer a really thin, New York-style slice where you can fold it in half. Have you ever tried making pizza dough at home? It's really hard to get the yeast to activate properly if the water isn't warm enough, but if it's too hot, you just kill the yeast entirely. I bought this little digital thermometer just to measure the water temperature for baking. It's the same kind we use in the lab, actually, just a cheaper brand. But yeah, I don't just sit around feeling anxious if there's nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Unable to experience pleasure? He moved to Chicago, which is crazy because the winters there are brutal. The wind coming off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you. They call it the Lake Effect. It actually has to do with the temperature differential between the relatively warm lake water and the freezing arctic air moving over it. It creates these massive bands of snow that just dump feet of powder on the city in a matter of hours. The city has this massive fleet of salt trucks and snowplows they deploy, and you can actually track them in real time on this app the city made. I was looking into getting one made from TPE, thermoplastic elastomers. TPE is cool because it has the elasticity of rubber but you can process it like plastic, meaning it can be melted down and recycled. Injection molding TPE is kind of tricky, though, because you have to get the shear rate just right in the mold or the whole thing warps. We were doing some injection molding tests at work a while back with this massive hydraulic press, and the hydraulic fluid actually started leaking. It was a huge hazard to clean up, we had to use all these special absorbent booms and granular clay just to contain it before it reached the drains. The clay is basically just industrial-grade kitty litter, if you think about it. It's highly porous, so it traps the oil molecules through capillary action. \n\nBut yeah... most days, I just don't have the desire to do any of the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're depressed?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't know... I guess? I mean, I just feel so flat and empty all the time. It's like someone just turned the contrast down on my whole life. The contrast on my monitor at work actually got messed up the other day, the black levels were completely crushed. I had to recalibrate the whole display using a colorimeter. It's this little optical device that you hang over the screen, and it measures the exact wavelength and intensity of the light being emitted. The software then generates a custom ICC profile to correct the gamma curve and color temperature. It's super important when you're looking at CAD models all day, otherwise the rendering shadows look completely wrong and you can't see the fine tolerances. We work with tolerances down to the thousandth of an inch, so you really need to be able to see what you're doing. If a part is even slightly out of spec, the whole assembly can fail. We had a bearing fail on a prototype last month because the shaft was machined just a hair too wide, and the friction just tore the whole thing apart. The heat buildup was intense, it actually blued the steel. Steel turns blue at around 550 degrees Fahrenheit due to a thin oxide layer forming on the surface. It's a process called tempering, which usually makes the steel less brittle, but in a bearing, it just means you've completely ruined the temper and the part is going to seize up. But anyway... yeah, I guess depressed is a word for it. I just feel like I'm dragging myself through every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild or moderate?\n\nPatient: Mild or moderate? Um... I guess moderate? It's definitely not mild, because it's just... it's taking over everything. It's like this heavy weight that just sits on me all day. Weight distribution is actually a huge problem we're trying to solve at work right now with this new chassis design. The center of gravity is way too high, so if you take a corner at anything over, like, twenty miles an hour, the lateral forces want to flip the whole thing over. We're looking into using a denser alloy for the base plate to bring the CG down, maybe something with a higher tungsten content. Tungsten is incredibly dense, almost the same density as gold, which is why people sometimes use it to make fake gold bars. They just plate a tungsten core with real gold, and it passes the weight test. You have to use ultrasound or drill into it to figure out it's a fake. Ultrasound testing is pretty fascinating, actually. It uses high-frequency sound waves that bounce back when they hit a boundary between different materials, so you can see the internal structure without destroying the object. We use a similar non-destructive testing method for checking welds on the high-pressure tanks, but we use X-ray radiography instead. The X-rays pass right through the metal, and any voids or cracks show up as darker spots on the film because less radiation is absorbed there. It's just like getting an X-ray at the dentist, really, just with way higher energy levels. You have to wear these heavy lead aprons and stand behind a shielded wall when the machine is running. But yeah, moderate. It's definitely not just a mild thing, it's pretty bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And even doing yoga or walking in the woods... it just feels like another task I have to do instead of something I actually want to do. \n\nI actually need a new yoga mat anyway, the one I have is made of PVC, polyvinyl chloride, which is honestly terrible for the environment because it takes centuries to degrade. I was looking into getting one made from TPE, thermoplastic elastomers. TPE is cool because it has the elasticity of rubber but you can process it like plastic, meaning it can be melted down and recycled. Injection molding TPE is kind of tricky, though, because you have to get the shear rate just right in the mold or the whole thing warps. We were doing some injection molding tests at work a while back with this massive hydraulic press, and the hydraulic fluid actually started leaking. It was a huge hazard to clean up, we had to use all these special absorbent booms and granular clay just to contain it before it reached the drains. The clay is basically just industrial-grade kitty litter, if you think about it. It's highly porous, so it traps the oil molecules through capillary action. \n\nBut yeah... most days, I just don't have the desire to do any of the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're depressed?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't know... I guess? I mean, I just feel so flat and empty all the time. It's like someone just turned the contrast down on my whole life. The contrast on my monitor at work actually got messed up the other day, the black levels were completely crushed. I had to recalibrate the whole display using a colorimeter. It's this little optical device that you hang over the screen, and it measures the exact wavelength and intensity of the light being emitted. The software then generates a custom ICC profile to correct the gamma curve and color temperature. It's super important when you're looking at CAD models all day, otherwise the rendering shadows look completely wrong and you can't see the fine tolerances. We work with tolerances down to the thousandth of an inch, so you really need to be able to see what you're doing. If a part is even slightly out of spec, the whole assembly can fail. We had a bearing fail on a prototype last month because the shaft was machined just a hair too wide, and the friction just tore the whole thing apart. The heat buildup was intense, it actually blued the steel. Steel turns blue at around 550 degrees Fahrenheit due to a thin oxide layer forming on the surface. It's a process called tempering, which usually makes the steel less brittle, but in a bearing, it just means you've completely ruined the temper and the part is going to seize up. But anyway... yeah, I guess depressed is a word for it. I just feel like I'm dragging myself through every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild or moderate?\n\nPatient: Mild or moderate? Um... I guess moderate? It's definitely not mild, because it's just... it's taking over everything. It's like this heavy weight that just sits on me all day. Weight distribution is actually a huge problem we're trying to solve at work right now with this new chassis design. The center of gravity is way too high, so if you take a corner at anything over, like, twenty miles an hour, the lateral forces want to flip the whole thing over. We're looking into using a denser alloy for the base plate to bring the CG down, maybe something with a higher tungsten content. Tungsten is incredibly dense, almost the same density as gold, which is why people sometimes use it to make fake gold bars. They just plate a tungsten core with real gold, and it passes the weight test. You have to use ultrasound or drill into it to figure out it's a fake. Ultrasound testing is pretty fascinating, actually. It uses high-frequency sound waves that bounce back when they hit a boundary between different materials, so you can see the internal structure without destroying the object. We use a similar non-destructive testing method for checking welds on the high-pressure tanks, but we use X-ray radiography instead. The X-rays pass right through the metal, and any voids or cracks show up as darker spots on the film because less radiation is absorbed there. It's just like getting an X-ray at the dentist, really, just with way higher energy levels. You have to wear these heavy lead aprons and stand behind a shielded wall when the machine is running. But yeah, moderate. It's definitely not just a mild thing, it's pretty bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
How long? Um... well, this has been going on since I was moved into the prototype-testing role at work. That was about a year and a month ago. Prototype testing is honestly such a stressful department because you're constantly dealing with things that are designed to fail, or at least, you're trying to find out *how* they fail. We have this one rig that just does fatigue testing on suspension springs. It just compresses and releases the spring, like, thousands of times an hour. The noise is unbelievable, it's this constant rhythmic thudding that just vibrates right through the floorboards and into your teeth. We had to install these specialized acoustic dampening panels all over the walls of that testing bay. They're made of this open-cell polyurethane foam, which is great for absorbing high-frequency sound waves, but it's completely useless for low-frequency vibrations. For that, you need mass-loaded vinyl, which is basically just a heavy sheet of PVC infused with barium sulfate to give it extra density. Barium sulfate is actually the same stuff they make you drink when you get a GI tract X-ray, because it's radio-opaque, meaning X-rays can't pass through it, so it makes your intestines light up on the scan. It tastes like absolute chalk, though. My brother had to drink it once when he swallowed a Lego tire when we were kids. But yeah, it's been about thirteen months of feeling like this.

I always thought it would be cool to make clothes. I tried sewing a pillowcase once, but the machine kept jamming. It was my roommate's machine, a Singer, I think. The software then generates a custom ICC profile to correct the gamma curve and color temperature. It's super important when you're looking at CAD models all day, otherwise the rendering shadows look completely wrong and you can't see the fine tolerances. The center of gravity is way too high, so if you take a corner at anything over, like, twenty miles an hour, the lateral forces want to flip the whole thing over. We're looking into using a denser alloy for the base plate to bring the CG down, maybe something with a higher tungsten content. Tungsten is incredibly dense, almost the same density as gold, which is why people sometimes use it to make fake gold bars. They just plate a tungsten core with real gold, and it passes the weight test. You have to use ultrasound or drill into it to figure out it's a fake. Ultrasound testing is pretty fascinating, actually. It uses high-frequency sound waves that bounce back when they hit a boundary between different materials, so you can see the internal structure without destroying the object. We use a similar non-destructive testing method for checking welds on the high-pressure tanks, but we use X-ray radiography instead. The X-rays pass right through the metal, and any voids or cracks show up as darker spots on the film because less radiation is absorbed there. It's just like getting an X-ray at the dentist, really, just with way higher energy levels. You have to wear these heavy lead aprons and stand behind a shielded wall when the machine is running. Almond milk is so expensive now, though. I tried making my own oat milk once, you just blend oats and water and strain it through a cheesecloth. But it came out really slimy. Apparently you're not supposed to squeeze the bag too hard. So yeah, I haven't really noticed any weight changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the day, every day?\n\nPatient: Anxious most of the day? Um... I don't really think so. I mean, not all day every day. Sometimes I feel a little on edge, I guess, but it's not a constant thing. Like yesterday, I was a little stressed because the Wi-Fi in my apartment kept dropping. I work from home a lot, doing CAD models and stuff for this engineering firm, and it's impossible when the connection is spotty. I called the internet company, and I was on hold for like forty-five minutes. They play that same looping jazz music the whole time. It's supposed to be calming, I think, but it just gets stuck in your head. It kind of reminded me of the music they play in the elevators at my dentist's office. My dentist has this huge aquarium in the waiting room, it's salt water, which is really hard to maintain. You have to check the salinity levels constantly. He has these clownfish in there, and a couple of tangs. I always wanted a fish tank when I was a kid, but my mom said they were too much work. We had a hamster instead. His name was Barnaby. He used to run on his wheel all night long, it squeaked so loud. My dad tried putting WD-40 on it, but it didn't really help. I think hamsters are nocturnal anyway, so it makes sense he was up. But yeah, no, I wouldn't say I'm anxious all the time. Just normal stuff, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're always on the lookout for potential problems or dangers, even when things seem to be going well?\n\nPatient: Always on the lookout? No, not really. I don't walk around expecting bad things to happen. I mean, at work I have to look for potential problems, but that's just because I'm an engineering tech. We do a lot of stress testing on these composite materials to make sure they don't micro-fracture under pressure. We use this giant hydraulic press for it, and it's so incredibly loud when it runs. I actually had to buy special noise-canceling headphones just to sit at my desk. I got the Sony ones, and they have this feature where if you start talking, the music pauses automatically. It's supposed to be helpful, but it's really annoying when I'm just humming to myself and the audio cuts out. I've been listening to a lot of 90s alternative lately. Did you know cassette tapes are actually making a huge comeback? My brother just bought a refurbished Walkman on eBay for like two hundred dollars. It's crazy how much people pay for vintage tech right now. He had to open it up and replace the drive belt inside because the original rubber had completely dry-rotted. He tried using a regular office rubber band at first, but it made the music sound all warbly and distorted. But yeah, outside of checking CAD models and materials at work, I'm not really looking over my shoulder for danger or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things for no apparent reason?\n\nPatient: Worrying for no reason? Um... no, I don't think I do that. If I'm worried, there's usually a reason for it. Like, I worry about getting my projects done on time, but that's normal, right? We have these really strict deadlines because the manufacturing team needs the specs before they can calibrate the CNC machines. Those machines are amazing, honestly. They run on G-code, which is this really old programming language from the fifties, I think. It basically just tells the drill bits exactly where to move on an X, Y, and Z axis. My friend Sarah took a whole class on it in college. We went to the same university, actually. They had this massive library with a glass roof, and when it rained, it sounded like you were inside a drum. It was so peaceful to study there, except during finals week when people would literally sleep on the couches. One time I saw a guy bring a whole coffee maker and plug it in right next to the reference section. He had a French press and everything. I love French press coffee, but it always leaves that little bit of grit at the bottom of the mug. You have to use a really coarse grind, otherwise it just pushes right through the metal filter. I tried using my regular drip coffee grounds once and it was basically mud. So yeah, I don't really sit around worrying about nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're depressed most of the time?\n\nPatient: Depressed most of the time? Um... I don't know about most of the time. I definitely feel down sometimes, but who doesn't? I guess lately it's been a little heavier, maybe. Like, it's hard to get out of bed some mornings. My bed is really comfortable, though. I just bought this memory foam mattress topper online because my old mattress was getting this huge dip in the middle. It came rolled up in this tiny box, and when you cut the plastic, it just expands super fast. It smelled like chemicals for like three days, though. I had to leave my bedroom window open, which was freezing because the heat in my building is controlled by the landlord. He keeps it at like 65 degrees, which is barely legal, I think. I tried talking to him about it, but he's always \"busy\" fixing the plumbing in the basement. It's an old building, pre-war, so the pipes are probably cast iron and rusting out. I read somewhere that cast iron pipes can last a hundred years, but eventually, the minerals in the water build up and choke off the flow. It's called tuberculation. It's actually a huge problem in city water mains. They have to send these special robots down into the pipes to scrape them clean. I saw a documentary about it on YouTube. The robots have these rotating wire brushes... but yeah, I don't think I'm depressed all the time. Just tired, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're frequently anxious, even when there's nothing in particular to be anxious about?\n\nPatient: Anxious for no reason? Um... no, not really. I had to do a presentation last month on the tensile strength of this new carbon fiber weave we were testing. Carbon fiber is so tricky to work with because if you don't cure the epoxy resin at the exact right temperature, it gets brittle. We have this massive industrial oven just for curing it. It looks like a giant pizza oven, honestly. It actually made me crave pizza the whole time I was running the tests. I ended up ordering from this local place down the street, but they use way too much cornmeal on the bottom of the crust. It gets all over your hands and the table. I prefer a really thin, New York-style slice where you can fold it in half. Have you ever tried making pizza dough at home? It's really hard to get the yeast to activate properly if the water isn't warm enough, but if it's too hot, you just kill the yeast entirely. I bought this little digital thermometer just to measure the water temperature for baking. It's the same kind we use in the lab, actually, just a cheaper brand. But yeah, I don't just sit around feeling anxious if there's nothing going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Unable to experience pleasure? Um... yeah, I guess I've noticed that lately. Like, my 3D printer for miniatures has been sitting with the exact same half-finished figure on the build plate for months now. It's a resin printer, which gets way better detail for small models than the standard filament ones, but it's a huge mess to clean up. You have to wash the prints in 99% isopropyl alcohol to get the uncured resin off before you put it in the UV curing station. I buy the alcohol in these massive gallon jugs online because it's way cheaper. The delivery guy always looks at me funny when he drops off like four gallons of highly flammable liquid on my porch. He drives one of those new electric delivery vans, which are actually super quiet. I almost stepped out in front of one the other day because I didn't hear an engine. They use regenerative braking, so when they take their foot off the accelerator, the motor acts like a generator and charges the battery while slowing the van down. It's brilliant engineering, honestly, catching that kinetic energy instead of losing it as heat in the brake pads, but it makes the trucks completely stealthy. You really have to watch where you're walking. But anyway, yeah, I just haven't had the drive to deal with the printer or paint anything. And even other stuff, like doing yoga or going for a walk in the woods... it just feels like another task I have to get done instead of something I actually want to do. I just don't have the motivation for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy even the smallest pleasures in life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Smallest pleasures? I mean... It's supposed to be a paladin for a tabletop campaign my friend was trying to put together, but the campaign never even started because our dungeon master got a new job. He moved to Chicago, which is crazy because the winters there are brutal. The wind coming off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you. They call it the Lake Effect. It actually has to do with the temperature differential between the relatively warm lake water and the freezing arctic air moving over it. It creates these massive bands of snow that just dump feet of powder on the city in a matter of hours. The city has this massive fleet of salt trucks and snowplows they deploy, and you can actually track them in real time on this app the city made. It's kind of mesmerizing to watch all the little truck icons moving around the map, clearing the grid. I downloaded the app once when I visited him just to see how it worked. But yeah, even just little things... like a walk in the woods or whatever, it just feels like a chore now. I just don't want to do any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to enjoy things you used to find pleasurable most days?\n\nPatient: Wait, are you just reading from a checklist or something? And even doing yoga or walking in the woods... it just feels like another task I have to do instead of something I actually want to do. \n\nI actually need a new yoga mat anyway, the one I have is made of PVC, polyvinyl chloride, which is honestly terrible for the environment because it takes centuries to degrade. I was looking into getting one made from TPE, thermoplastic elastomers. TPE is cool because it has the elasticity of rubber but you can process it like plastic, meaning it can be melted down and recycled. Injection molding TPE is kind of tricky, though, because you have to get the shear rate just right in the mold or the whole thing warps. We were doing some injection molding tests at work a while back with this massive hydraulic press, and the hydraulic fluid actually started leaking. It was a huge hazard to clean up, we had to use all these special absorbent booms and granular clay just to contain it before it reached the drains. The clay is basically just industrial-grade kitty litter, if you think about it. It's highly porous, so it traps the oil molecules through capillary action. \n\nBut yeah... most days, I just don't have the desire to do any of the things I used to like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're depressed?\n\nPatient: Depressed? I don't know... I guess? I mean, I just feel so flat and empty all the time. It's like someone just turned the contrast down on my whole life. The contrast on my monitor at work actually got messed up the other day, the black levels were completely crushed. I had to recalibrate the whole display using a colorimeter. It's this little optical device that you hang over the screen, and it measures the exact wavelength and intensity of the light being emitted. The software then generates a custom ICC profile to correct the gamma curve and color temperature. It's super important when you're looking at CAD models all day, otherwise the rendering shadows look completely wrong and you can't see the fine tolerances. We work with tolerances down to the thousandth of an inch, so you really need to be able to see what you're doing. If a part is even slightly out of spec, the whole assembly can fail. We had a bearing fail on a prototype last month because the shaft was machined just a hair too wide, and the friction just tore the whole thing apart. The heat buildup was intense, it actually blued the steel. Steel turns blue at around 550 degrees Fahrenheit due to a thin oxide layer forming on the surface. It's a process called tempering, which usually makes the steel less brittle, but in a bearing, it just means you've completely ruined the temper and the part is going to seize up. But anyway... yeah, I guess depressed is a word for it. I just feel like I'm dragging myself through every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild or moderate?\n\nPatient: Mild or moderate? Um... I guess moderate? It's definitely not mild, because it's just... it's taking over everything. It's like this heavy weight that just sits on me all day. Weight distribution is actually a huge problem we're trying to solve at work right now with this new chassis design. The center of gravity is way too high, so if you take a corner at anything over, like, twenty miles an hour, the lateral forces want to flip the whole thing over. We're looking into using a denser alloy for the base plate to bring the CG down, maybe something with a higher tungsten content. Tungsten is incredibly dense, almost the same density as gold, which is why people sometimes use it to make fake gold bars. They just plate a tungsten core with real gold, and it passes the weight test. You have to use ultrasound or drill into it to figure out it's a fake. Ultrasound testing is pretty fascinating, actually. It uses high-frequency sound waves that bounce back when they hit a boundary between different materials, so you can see the internal structure without destroying the object. We use a similar non-destructive testing method for checking welds on the high-pressure tanks, but we use X-ray radiography instead. The X-rays pass right through the metal, and any voids or cracks show up as darker spots on the film because less radiation is absorbed there. It's just like getting an X-ray at the dentist, really, just with way higher energy levels. You have to wear these heavy lead aprons and stand behind a shielded wall when the machine is running. But yeah, moderate. It's definitely not just a mild thing, it's pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: How long? Um... well, this has been going on since I was moved into the prototype-testing role at work. That was about a year and a month ago. Prototype testing is honestly such a stressful department because you're constantly dealing with things that are designed to fail, or at least, you're trying to find out *how* they fail. We have this one rig that just does fatigue testing on suspension springs. It just compresses and releases the spring, like, thousands of times an hour. The noise is unbelievable, it's this constant rhythmic thudding that just vibrates right through the floorboards and into your teeth. We had to install these specialized acoustic dampening panels all over the walls of that testing bay. They're made of this open-cell polyurethane foam, which is great for absorbing high-frequency sound waves, but it's completely useless for low-frequency vibrations. For that, you need mass-loaded vinyl, which is basically just a heavy sheet of PVC infused with barium sulfate to give it extra density. Barium sulfate is actually the same stuff they make you drink when you get a GI tract X-ray, because it's radio-opaque, meaning X-rays can't pass through it, so it makes your intestines light up on the scan. It tastes like absolute chalk, though. My brother had to drink it once when he swallowed a Lego tire when we were kids. But yeah, it's been about thirteen months of feeling like this."
